It falls short of a 7 because a user relying on VoiceOver cannot operate it at all — the Review screen is ~1,100 ungrouped stops and no outcome is ever announced — there is zero Dynamic Type support (91 fixed point sizes vs 8 semantic styles, 17 of them under 11pt), zero focus treatment despite the roadmap listing visible focus as shipped, and the button that moves 30 GB to Trash is pixel-identical to the button that starts a read-only scan. A stranger would find it attractive and trustworthy on first look and then hit the ceiling at 126 un-batch-selectable rows.",
  "summary": "Judged as a product rather than as code, ViggoClean's decision surface is unusually well-built and its presentation shell is unusually generic. The parts that carry consequence — manual-only selection, a confirmation sheet listing every exact full path with an acknowledgement checkbox and deliberately no Return-key default action, selection that survives filtering correctly, and four separate refusals to call a Trash move \\\"freed space\\\" — are better than what commercial Mac cleaners ship, and 8 of the 10 UI states the roadmap specifies are genuinely implemented in views. Reduce Motion is the standout: all 24 motion sites are correctly guarded, so the docs' claim survives verification, which is rare. Against that, accessibility beyond motion is thin to the point of exclusion: 8 accessibility modifiers total, no announcements so a VoiceOver user gets silence after confirming a 30 GB delete, ungrouped 9-stop rows across a 126-item list, zero Dynamic Type (91 fixed sizes vs 8 semantic styles, 17 under 11pt), and no focus indication at all despite the roadmap listing it as shipped. Two things undercut the honesty the rest of the app works hard for: the confirmation omits the `consequence` text the app already computes, and failures render in the same friendly mint info banner as successes. Visually it is coherent and attractive but reads as a Linear-style web dashboard — hand-rolled sidebar, hidden title bar, forced dark mode, a marketing hero on every screen, no NavigationSplitView, no Table, no Settings scene — which in 2026 is also exactly what every AI-assisted SwiftUI project looks like. The single highest-leverage change is replacing the card list with a real `Table`: sortable columns, native multi-select and shift-click range would fix the biggest workflow gap and make the app read as native in one move."

## a0a512face633903b

{
  "summary": "I ran ViggoClean's real scanner (ViggoCleanCore sources compiled verbatim into a read-only probe; no cleanup, nothing trashed) against this machine — an actual 512 GB dev Mac, 460 GiB volume, 350 GiB used, 81% full. ## aac8e0a2ff794b0d6

{
  "summary": "On the narrow question of Swift language and concurrency correctness, this is the strongest part of the project and it is genuinely better than most professional Swift code I see. I built the package in true Swift 6 language mode (`swiftLanguageMode(.v6)` on all five targets in a scratch copy) and under `-strict-concurrency=complete`: zero errors, zero warnings, and all 32 tests still pass. Across 2,419 lines of Swift there is not one `try!`, `as!`, force unwrap, implicitly-unwrapped optional, `fatalError`, `@unchecked Sendable`, `nonisolated(unsafe)`, `DispatchQueue`, or semaphore. I empirically verified the hard part — that cancellation really reaches the detached scanner worker — and it does, in about 1 ms. The recursive `walk` in `FileSafety.snapshot` is safe in practice: I built a 480-level tree (984-byte path, near PATH_MAX) and snapshotted it on a cooperative-pool thread with no stack overflow, so that theoretical concern does not materialise. Where the code is actually weak is one level up: API-level correctness and observability. `FileSafety.snapshot` performs three metadata round-trips per file — one of them provably redundant — making it ~15x slower than a correctly-configured `FileManager.enumerator` (measured: 2.9 s vs 0.19 s for 30,000 files), which directly hurts the author's own use case of multi-hundred-thousand-file Xcode/Gradle caches. Every error is flattened to a `localizedDescription` string, so the app structurally cannot tell a permissions failure from a changed-file failure, and there is no logging of any kind.",
  "score": 7.5,
  "scoreRationale": "Comparison class: solo-developer / small-team indie macOS utilities written in Swift. On the pure concurrency-model axis this is a 9 — Swift 6 strict-concurrency clean with no escape hatches, a correct and empirically-verified `withTaskCancellationHandler` bridge into a detached worker, an operation-ID guard that defeats stale progress callbacks, an `actor` for the history store, and `@MainActor`-annotated protocols that push isolation into the contract rather than the implementation. Most shipping indie Swift apps I would call \"solid\" are still in Swift 5 mode with `@unchecked Sendable` sprinkled around; this is not. It is pulled down to 7.5 by API-level correctness, which sits around 6: a real, measurable FileManager misuse that costs ~15x on the exact workload the app exists to handle, systematic erasure of error types into localized strings (which forecloses the single most useful feature a disk cleaner can have — telling the user *why* macOS refused), a presenter that rebuilds the entire view state on every progress tick and keystroke, zero automated coverage of every OS-touching adapter, and no logging at all. It is not a 9 because the shipping Xcode target is still `SWIFT_VERSION = 5.0` with no strict-concurrency setting and there is no CI, so nothing actually preserves the excellent state the code is currently in.",

(8,49 sec)\", no diagnostics. Grep across Sources/ for `try!|as!|fatalError|IUO|@unchecked|nonisolated(unsafe)|DispatchQueue|DispatchSemaphore|Thread\\.` returns zero matches.       "title": "Cancellation genuinely propagates from the ViewModel through the service into the detached scanner worker — verified empirically at ~1 ms latency",
      "severity": "STRENGTH",
      "location": "Sources/ViggoClean/Services/FileSystemServices.swift:8-15; Sources/ViggoCleanPresentation/WorkspaceViewModel.swift:118-122",
      "evidence": "`Task.detached` does not inherit cancellation, and the code correctly bridges it:\n```swift\nlet worker = Task.detached(priority: .userInitiated) {\n    try DiskScanner().scan(configuration: configuration, progress: progress)\n}\nreturn try await withTaskCancellationHandler {\n    let report = try await worker.value\n    try Task.checkCancellation()\n    return report\n} onCancel: { worker.cancel() }\n```\nI reproduced this service against a synthetic 30,000-file cache tree and cancelled the outer task 150 ms in: `CANCEL_PROBE: CancellationError after 0.1564s` — i.e. the synchronous `Task.checkCancellation()` calls inside `DiskScanner.scan` (Scanner.swift:39,102,121,129) and `FileSafety.snapshot` (FileSafety.swift:42) fire within ~6 ms of `.cancel()`. Separately, the stale-callback guard is correct and non-obvious:\n```swift\nTask { @MainActor [weak self] in\n    guard let self, self.scanID == id, self.session.operation == .scanning else { return }\n```\nand is actually tested (WorkspaceViewModelTests.swift:101-103 fires a stale `progress` callback and asserts the UI does not change).",
      "impact": "Cancel actually cancels, immediately, instead of leaving an orphaned worker hammering the disk — which is the failure mode almost every hobby Swift app with a background scanner has.       "title": "FileSafety.snapshot does three metadata round-trips per file — one of them provably redundant — making it ~15x slower than a prefetching FileManager.enumerator, on exactly the huge developer caches this app exists to clean",
      "severity": "HIGH",
      "location": "Sources/ViggoCleanCore/FileSafety.swift:73-81 (walk), with record() at 41-69",
      "evidence": "`walk` calls `record(entry)`, which stats the entry at line 43, and then immediately stats the same entry again at line 75:\n```swift\nfunc walk(_ entry: URL) throws {\n    try record(entry)                                          // line 43: attributesOfItem(atPath:)\n    let attrs = try fm.attributesOfItem(atPath: entry.path)     // line 75: the SAME stat, again\n    if attrs[.type] as? FileAttributeType == .typeDirectory {\n```\nPlus a third round-trip at line 51 (`entry.resourceValues(forKeys: [.fileAllocatedSizeKey, .isPackageKey])`). Measured on a synthetic 30,000-file tree, release build, warm cache, 3 runs each:\n- current `FileSafety.snapshot`: 3.28 / 2.91 / 2.87 s\n- identical recursion with ONE `attributesOfItem` per entry: 1.30 / 1.41 / 1.49 s (the duplicate stat alone costs ~2x)\n- `FileManager.enumerator(at:includingPropertiesForKeys:)` with the keys prefetched: 0.191 / 0.183 / 0.190 s (~15x faster overall)",
      "impact": "~10,300 files/second. A developer's Xcode DerivedData or Gradle cache routinely holds 300,000-800,000 files, so one snapshot of it is roughly 30-80 seconds — and the snapshot runs TWICE per cleaned item (Scanner.swift:49 during scan, then again at FileSafety.swift:103 for revalidation before Trash). That is one to three minutes of pure stat churn to clean a single DerivedData folder, on cold cache considerably worse. This is the app's core workload and the one the author personally has. It is a self-inflicted cost from API choice, not an inherent cost of the safety guarantees — `enumerator` with prefetched resource keys can enforce every one of the same refusals (symlink, package, cross-device, hard link).",
      "alreadyDisclosed": true,
      "recommendation": "Quick win first (minutes): delete line 75 and have `record` return the `FileAttributeType` it already computed at line 44 — that is a free ~2x with zero behaviour change. Keep the sort for digest stability by sorting the collected lines before hashing rather than sorting each directory. Note docs/ARCHITECTURE.md:68 discloses that \"very broad trees may be slow\" — true, but it does not disclose that the implementation is 15x off the right API, and the duplicate stat at line 75 is a plain bug, not an accepted trade-off.",

      "title": "Project roots and exclusions are persisted as raw path strings rather than security-scoped bookmarks, which makes the App Sandbox — and therefore the Mac App Store — structurally unreachable without redesigning persistence",
      "severity": "HIGH",
      "location": "Sources/ViggoClean/Services/PersistenceServices.swift:9-17; Sources/ViggoClean/Services/MacDesktopService.swift:7-15",
      "evidence": "```swift\nfunc load() -> WorkspaceSettings {\n    WorkspaceSettings(\n        projectRoots: (defaults.stringArray(forKey: \"projectRoots\") ?? []).map { URL(fileURLWithPath: $0) },\n        exclusions: (defaults.stringArray(forKey: \"exclusions\") ?? []).map { URL(fileURLWithPath: $0) })\n}\nfunc save(_ settings: WorkspaceSettings) throws {\n    defaults.set(settings.projectRoots.map(\\.path), forKey: \"projectRoots\")\n```\nThe folder access is granted by `NSOpenPanel` (MacDesktopService.swift:7-15) but only the `.path` string survives; `URL.bookmarkData(options: .withSecurityScope)` and `startAccessingSecurityScopedResource()` appear nowhere in the codebase.",
      "impact": "Answers the author's store question directly. Under the App Sandbox, the powerbox grant from NSOpenPanel lasts only for the process lifetime — a reconstructed `URL(fileURLWithPath:)` carries no entitlement. Every saved project root would silently stop being scannable the moment the app is relaunched, with no error the user can interpret. Separately and more fundamentally, a sandboxed app cannot read other applications' `~/Library/Caches` subdirectories at all, which is where most of this app's value lives. So Mac App Store distribution is not a packaging step away — it would gut the product. Direct distribution (Developer ID + notarization + a DMG) is the only realistic channel, and the persistence code is fine as-is for that. docs/ROADMAP.md:156 does flag \"Shqyrtim i veçantë i sandbox/entitlements dhe kanalit të shpërndarjes\" as future work, but it does not record that the current persistence design is incompatible with the sandbox.",
      "alreadyDisclosed": true,
      "recommendation": "Do not target the Mac App Store. Ship via Developer ID + notarization + Sparkle, which requires no change here. Record this conclusion in ROADMAP §10 so the decision is not re-litigated later.",

      "blocksShipping": false

    {
      "title": "publish() rebuilds the entire ViewState on every progress tick and every search keystroke, giving O(n^2) main-actor work that is fine at today's ~126 candidates but cliffs hard as the roadmap's broader rules land",
      "severity": "MEDIUM",
      "location": "Sources/ViggoCleanPresentation/WorkspaceViewModel.swift:119-122 and 163-165; Sources/ViggoCleanPresentation/WorkspacePresenter.swift:31-63",
      "evidence": "Every progress callback from the scanner (`progress(title)` fires once per `add()` — Scanner.swift:47, i.e. once per candidate) lands in:\n```swift\nTask { @MainActor [weak self] in\n    guard let self, self.scanID == id, self.session.operation == .scanning else { return }\n    self.session.progress = name; self.publish()\n}\n```\nand `publish()` is a full rebuild: `WorkspacePresenter.make` maps every candidate through `row()` (WorkspacePresenter.swift:48-51), recomputes all five category aggregates (31-38), and re-renders the whole history list (56-61). `byteLabel` (Models.swift:105) calls `ByteCountFormatter.string(fromByteCount:countStyle:)` roughly five times per row. Measured cost of a single `WorkspacePresenter.make`, release build:\n- n=100: 0.65 ms (n publishes = 0.06 s total)\n- n=500: 2.82 ms (1.41 s)\n- n=2000: 10.91 ms (21.8 s)\n- n=5000: 26.35 ms (131.8 s)",
      "impact": "At the 126 candidates the author measured in docs/VALIDATION.md:27 this is invisible — under 1 ms. But it is quadratic: at ~1,500 candidates a single publish exceeds the 16.7 ms frame budget, so the scan progress line itself starts dropping frames, and cumulative main-actor work during one scan reaches ~22 s at 2,000 candidates. The more immediately visible symptom is search: `.queryChanged` also calls `publish()`, so every keystroke in the Review filter costs a full rebuild plus a SwiftUI diff of the entire list. ROADMAP phases 2 and 3 (browser adapters, AI tool caches, installers, Downloads, app leftovers) will push candidate counts into exactly this range. There is also no ordering guarantee between the spawned `Task { @MainActor }` blocks, so progress labels can appear out of order.",
      "alreadyDisclosed": false,
      "recommendation": "Two independent fixes. (1) Decouple progress from state rebuilds: keep `progress` as its own `@Published` string updated directly, and do not call `publish()` from the progress path at all; or coalesce with an AsyncStream and throttle to ~10 Hz. (2) Make `publish()` cheap: cache a single `ByteCountFormatter` instance instead of calling the static convenience per cell, precompute each `CandidateRowState` once when the scan report is applied, and re-derive only the filtered id list on query/selection changes rather than remapping every row.",
      "effortToFix": "hours",
      "blocksShipping": false
    },

FileAttributeType == .typeDirectory {\n        let children = try fm.contentsOfDirectory(at: entry, includingPropertiesForKeys: nil)\n            .sorted { $0.lastPathComponent < $1.lastPathComponent }\n        for child in children { try walk(child) }\n    }\n}\n```\nUnbounded recursion, and unlike the project scan (Scanner.swift:132,158) there is no `depth < 8` / 20,000-directory cap. I built a 480-level deep tree (final path length 984 bytes, just under PATH_MAX of 1024) and ran `FileSafety.snapshot` on it from inside a `Task.detached(priority: .userInitiated)` — i.e. a cooperative-pool thread with the smaller non-main stack, exactly as production does: passed in 0.205 s, no overflow.",

Because every FileManager call here uses absolute paths, macOS PATH_MAX caps the reachable depth at roughly 480-500 levels with single-character directory names, and the measured stack usage at that depth is comfortably within budget. A tree deeper than PATH_MAX cannot be traversed with absolute URLs anyway — `contentsOfDirectory` would return ENAMETOOLONG, which the scanner already catches and reports as a skipped location (Scanner.swift:55). The residual concern is only stylistic: the code has no explicit bound, so the safety is accidental rather than designed, and it would stop being true if the traversal ever moved to relative-path or `openat`-based enumeration.",

      "effortToFix": "minutes",
      "blocksShipping": false
    }

But the app has evidently never been run against the workload it was built for. Running the real scanner on this machine recovered 1.89 GB while the 8.2 GB DerivedData — the exact target named in README.md:32 and ROADMAP.md:13 — was refused in 65 ms because it contains 502 packages and 181 symlinks, and the all-or-nothing refusal at FileSafety.swift:40-49 also knocks out the SwiftPM, Google, JetBrains, Spotify, Playwright and Bruno caches. Underneath that sit three measured scale defects: unbounded RSS growth of ~9.2 KB per entry that never releases (170 MB, then 328 MB, then 485 MB over three passes on one 17k-entry tree; a fix with autoreleasepool measured 154 MB -> 31.6 MB and plateauing), a snapshot that is 47x slower than a plain walk and runs twice per candidate, and a quadratic dedup guard costing 87 s at 5,000 candidates. The tests pass, but every fixture is a handful of 23-byte files and the whole Core suite runs in 22 ms, so no test could ever have surfaced any of this — the one genuine test-design gap is that nothing builds a realistic developer-cache tree. Architecturally the code is ready to grow except in one place: the rule catalogue is a three-field tuple inlined in a 145-line function with a single shared consequence string for all seven rules, which cannot carry the eight-field versioned catalogue ROADMAP.md Phase 2 specifies, so that needs extracting before Phase 2 rather than after."

  "scoreRationale": "Comparison class: consumer macOS disk cleaners (CleanMyMac, DaisyDisk's cleanup features, the long tail of App Store \\\"cleaner\\\" apps) plus competent solo-dev macOS utilities. Against that class this is an 8, not a 5 and not a 9. It clears 7 comfortably: I could not find a single undisclosed path to an unintended or unrecoverable deletion after reading every line of FileSafety, Scanner, LocalCleaningRepository, Models, the ViewModel and the confirmation UI, and after compiling the core standalone and running it against real caches. `trashItem` is the only destructive call in the codebase, `canTrash` is enforced at four mutually distrustful layers, the confirmation sheet shows full paths behind a mandatory acknowledgement, history is structurally incapable of being replayed as a delete (`clean` takes `[Candidate]`; history holds strings), preview mode is inert at four layers, and there is no Select All — which in this product category is the single most common cause of real-world accidents. It is not a 9 for three reasons. First, the three invariants that actually fire in the field — package rejection, cross-device rejection, the `com.apple.*` skip — have no test, and the package rule turned out to be the one deciding the app's entire behaviour on a real dev Mac. Second, the running-app protection the docs promise is silently disabled for the highest-traffic cache directories by a `name.contains(\\\".\\\")` heuristic. Third, and most important for calibration: a large share of this safety is bought by refusing to act at all, which is the cheapest kind. I measured it — DerivedData and SwiftPM are both refused on the author's own machine, leaving 553 MB cleanable out of the tens of GB the project exists to recover. A 9 or 10 would be a model that is both this safe and actually functional, which means per-entry package and symlink handling rather than abort-the-whole-tree.",
  "summary": "On the data-loss axis specifically, this is genuinely better than the commercial software it competes with, and I say that having looked hard for a way to break it. Permanent deletion is structurally impossible — one `trashItem` call, no `removeItem`, no shell, no root helper — and `SafetyTests.swift:74-81` proves the no-fallback property rather than asserting it in a comment. The containment logic is sound: `isInside` is component-wise on `standardized` (not a string prefix, and `../secret` is tested), and `validate` walks every ancestor with `attributesOfItem`, which is `lstat`-based, so a symlinked ancestor causes refusal instead of authorising its target. The scan-to-cleanup race is handled by re-snapshotting and re-validating immediately before the move, and the test suite includes the directory-swapped-for-a-symlink attack that most products in this category would fail. Exclusions are checked in both directions at scan time and again at cleanup time against current settings; history cannot be replayed as a delete because `clean` only accepts in-memory `Candidate` values. The one finding that matters is the inverse of a safety hole: the abort-the-whole-tree policy on packages and descendant symlinks is so broad that I measured it refusing Xcode DerivedData, SwiftPM, ms-playwright and JetBrains on the author's own machine, leaving 553 MB of the tens of GB this app exists to recover — and the descendant-symlink half of that policy guards a threat `trashItem` does not have, since a rename-based move never dereferences links inside the moved tree. Below that sit two real but recoverable defects: the running-app guard is keyed on whether a folder name contains a dot, which silently disables it for `Google`, `JetBrains`, `BraveSoftware` and `Docker Desktop`, and the browser classification matches bundle IDs that do not exist as cache directory names, so Chrome's cache is filed under App caches alongside two Android Studio trees in one indivisible candidate."
}

IN NO EVENT SHALL THE AUTHORS BE LIABLE' clause, and that clause is the primary liability shield for free software. Right now the author has none of it, for a tool that moves other people's files to Trash. Realistic exposure for a free tool given to a handful of friends is close to nil — nobody sues a friend over a lost Gradle cache. Realistic exposure changes materially the moment money changes hands: a paid tool creates a contractual relationship, and in the EU consumer-sales law makes blanket exclusions of liability partially unenforceable anyway, which is precisely why commercial cleaners ship a real EULA rather than relying on a licence header.",

You are responsible for your own backups.' in the Settings card next to the existing cleanup-policy list at Sources/ViggoClean/Views/WorkspaceScreens.swift:99-107, where it fits the existing voice. Apache-2.0 is the better pick if there is any chance of charging later, because it also grants a patent licence and has an explicit liability section. If money is ever charged, stop relying on a source licence and get a short EULA written for the jurisdiction where the sale happens — a licence file is not a consumer contract.",

      "impact": "The Mac App Store mandates the App Sandbox. The entire value proposition here — 'open it, press Scan, it already knows where DerivedData and the Gradle cache live' — is the exact behaviour the sandbox exists to prevent. This is why CleanMyMac is not on the Mac App Store and why the store's disk utilities are either read-only analysers or require the user to pick every folder manually.   "score": 4,
  "scoreRationale": "Comparison class: hobby macOS apps distributed outside the App Store. A typical one scores around 3 on this lens — no licence, ad-hoc signing, no CI, no update path. ViggoClean sits above that on two axes that genuinely matter: build reproducibility (zero dependencies, a content-addressed idempotent project generator at scripts/generate-project.py:11-17, a correct `set -euo pipefail` build script) and documentation honesty (docs/VALIDATION.md:37-43's per-version 'Not performed' section is something most commercial products do not produce). But it has literally none of the five things that constitute distribution readiness: no LICENSE, no Developer ID or notarization, no update channel, no diagnostics or support surface, no CI. Three of those are hard blockers for handing it even to friends — an ad-hoc-signed app simply will not open for them, and every rebuild resets their Full Disk Access. It does not score lower than 4 because nothing here is wrong, only absent, and the absences are cheap to close: the engineering underneath is well above the release plumbing wrapped around it. It cannot score higher because on this specific lens the honest answer is that the project was never built to leave one machine, which docs/ROADMAP.md:148-156 says outright.",
  "summary": "On engineering quality this is well above typical hobby work; on release, legal and support readiness it is close to zero, and the gap between those two facts is the whole story. The single hard blocker is signing: scripts/build-app.sh:12 signs ad-hoc, so the built app has `Signature=adhoc`, `TeamIdentifier=not set` and a designated requirement that is a bare cdhash — friends who receive the .app will hit Gatekeeper and cannot open it without a System Settings detour, and because the identity is a cdhash rather than a Team ID, every rebuild silently revokes their Full Disk Access and makes the next scan quietly find less. The Mac App Store is structurally closed to this design: `ENABLE_APP_SANDBOX = \\\"NO\\\"` at project.pbxproj:64 and :66 is deliberate and correct, because a sandboxed app cannot enumerate ~/Library/Caches or DerivedData at all — direct Developer ID distribution is the realistic channel, and the project is closer to that than it looks since hardened runtime is already enabled and there are zero third-party dependencies to sign. The legal position is weaker than the author probably realises: there is no LICENSE and no warranty disclaimer anywhere, which for a tool that moves other people's files to Trash is the cheapest serious gap in the repository, fixable in ten minutes. Support is the other structural hole — zero logging statements in 2,523 lines, no support contact, no diagnostics export, so the no-telemetry stance (which is right and worth keeping) has quietly become no-diagnosability, and a friend's failure report will be unanswerable. Two corrections to the briefing I was given: the pbxproj does explicitly set both ENABLE_APP_SANDBOX=NO and ENABLE_HARDENED_RUNTIME=YES on both configurations, and the history is two commits rather than one, though the second touches only .gitignore."
}
```

Grepping all 48 tracked files for `bookmark|securityScope|startAccessing|stopAccessing|sandbox|entitlement` returns zero hits in source — the only matches are `ENABLE_APP_SANDBOX = \"NO\"` in the pbxproj, an unrelated comment at FileSafety.swift:108, and docs/ROADMAP.md:156. So the auditor's negative claim is correct.\n\nEMPIRICAL TEST. I built a Swift probe, packaged it as an .app, ad-hoc signed it with `com.apple.security.app-sandbox` + `com.apple.security.files.user-selected.read-write`, and ran it against the same binary unsigned as a control:\n\n  BASELINE:  HOME_API: /Users/tloshi\n             LIST_OK: /Users/tloshi/AndroidStudioProjects/loshis/viggoclean -> 19 entries\n             CACHES(/Users/tloshi/Library/Caches): 138 entries\n\n  SANDBOXED: HOME_API: /Users/tloshi/Library/Containers/com.loshi.sbprobe/Data\n             LIST_FAIL: ... \"you don't have permission to view it.\"\n             STAT_OK: NSFileTypeDirectory\n             CACHES(/Users/tloshi/Library/Containers/com.loshi.sbprobe/Data/Library/Caches): 0 entries\n\nBoth prongs of the finding are confirmed directly. A reconstructed `URL(fileURLWithPath:)` cannot enumerate a directory outside the container, and the container redirection empties out the entire cache-scanning surface the app is built around.\n\nCONTROL-FLOW DETAIL THE AUDITOR MISSED (in their favor). Note `STAT_OK` — metadata reads still succeed under the sandbox. That means Scanner.swift:27-34 `exists()` returns true and FileSafety.swift:17 `attributesOfItem` succeeds, so the symlink walk in `validate` does not fail on permissions. The actual rejection comes earlier and harder: `isInside(url, root: home)` at FileSafety.swift:12. I ran the isInside logic in isolation with a container home and a real-home root — returns `false`, so `validate` throws `SafetyError.refused(\"The item is outside its approved scan folder.\")` for every persisted root at Scanner.swift:119 and for every newly picked folder at LocalCleaningRepository.swift:46.\n\nREFUTATION ATTEMPTS THAT FAILED. (1) No guard elsewhere re-acquires access on load — LocalCleaningRepository.load():26-35 calls `settingsStore.load()` and never re-validates or re-prompts. (2) No bookmark handling hidden in the view or presenter layer. (3) A `temporary-exception.files.home-relative-path` entitlement would not rescue this, because it does not un-redirect `homeDirectoryForCurrentUser` — the code would still need changing, which is the finding's point. (4) The one thing I did refute is the \"silent failure\" characterization, since OverviewView.swift:92-100 does render `report.issues`.\n\nCONCLUSION: mechanism confirmed, impact direction correct and if anything deeper than described, but this is a forward-looking architectural constraint on an unexercised distribution channel rather than a defect in the current non-sandboxed build."
}

The auditor's timings smelled like -Onone, and ViggoClean.xcodeproj/project.pbxproj:65 ships Release at SWIFT_OPTIMIZATION_LEVEL = \"-O\". I benchmarked the verbatim predicate both ways: debug 8.42 us/cmp, release 8.14 us/cmp. Essentially identical, because the cost is inside Foundation's URL.standardized/pathComponents (precompiled, non-inlinable), not in Swift the optimizer can touch.\n\nREFUTATION 2 - \"a guard elsewhere bounds n.\" FAILED. The `visited > 20_000` cap at Scanner.swift:132 is scoped to the `for root in c.projectRoots` loop and never applies to the log enumerator at Scanner.swift:100-114, which is uncapped. README.md:62 independently documents that cap as project-scan-only. The only other early exits in add() are excluded()/exists() at :40, the protected check at :41, and the logicalBytes > 0 check at :50 - none bound candidate count.\n\nREFUTATION 3 - \"the synthetic predicate misrepresents real behavior.\" FAILED AND BACKFIRED. I copied Scanner.swift and FileSafety.swift into a harness, verified byte-identical with diff, and ran the genuine DiskScanner().scan() end-to-end against synthetic ~/Library/Logs trees: n=500 -> 1.89s, n=1000 -> 6.88s, n=2000 -> 26.12s, n=5000 -> 158.26s. Growth fits n^1.9. The real code is SLOWER than the auditor's estimate (158s vs 87s at n=5000).\n\nCAUSATION ISOLATED. I patched only the dedup guard to a Set-based lookup, preserving semantics (exact-URL set plus a directory list for the parent-containment case). n=5000 dropped 158.26s -> 3.97s and n=2000 dropped 26.12s -> 1.79s, converting quadratic to linear. This proves the guard is the cause, not incidental I/O.\n\nMACHINE STATE CONFIRMED. This Mac has 216 .log files under ~/Library/Logs, 128 older than 30 days (~0.21s measured) - exactly matching the auditor's \"~120 old logs\" explanation for why the author never hit it. Note ~/Library/Logs holds 8,967 files total but only 216 are .log (4,285 .json, 3,908 .html), so the .log filter is restrictive; reaching 2,000+ requires a heavier accumulation than this machine (one JetBrains IDE version alone contributes 55).\n\nDOCS CLAIM CONFIRMED. docs/ARCHITECTURE.md:68 mentions broad-tree snapshot slowness and the project-discovery depth/directory cap but says nothing about candidate-count scaling. docs/ROADMAP.md:153 lists performance profiling as future work. README.md:62 documents only the project-scan cap.\n\nSEVERITY. HIGH is correct. It is an uncapped quadratic defect on the app's primary operation, worsening monotonically over time in precisely the target population (developers with full disks and years of accumulated logs), undocumented, and crossing user-visible thresholds early (6.9s at n=1000, 26s at n=2000). Not CRITICAL: no data-loss or security impact, the UI stays responsive, Cancel works, and typical users sit well below the pain threshold."
}

Apple Distribution certificates are only issued to paid Apple Developer Program members. You have a live membership and you ship apps already. What you do *not* have is a **Developer ID Application** certificate — `security find-certificate -a -c \"Developer ID\"` returns only the CA intermediates, no leaf. That cert is a free, five-minute create from a membership you already hold (Xcode → Settings → Accounts → Manage Certificates → + → Developer ID Application). `xcrun notarytool --version` → `1.1.3 (42)`, already installed. Your local copy carries only `com.apple.provenance`, no `com.apple.quarantine`, which is exactly why you have never seen this fail — you built it here. The moment it travels (AirDrop, zip, Slack, download) it gets quarantined and your friend is blocked. The kernel policy grants by path subtree; the sandbox does not categorically forbid other apps' caches, it forbids reading them *without an explicit grant of that path*. A security-scoped bookmark grants the same subtree the same way. Two more things survive sandboxing that I expected to break: `NSWorkspace.shared.runningApplications` returns 129 apps / 116 bundle IDs (so your running-owner safety guard at `MacDesktopService.swift:16-18` still works), and `volumeTotalCapacity`/`volumeAvailableCapacityForImportantUsage` report the **real** volume (494,384,795,648 / 81,292,612,168), not the container. I would not stake a submission on it.\n2. **The bookmark route (the DevCleaner model) means real rework.** `PersistenceServices.swift:11-16` stores bare path strings (`defaults.set(settings.projectRoots.map(\\.path), ...)`). Under sandbox those are inert on relaunch — the powerbox grant dies with the process. You'd need security-scoped bookmark data, `startAccessingSecurityScopedResource()`, and stale-bookmark refresh. Bounded, but it's the persistence layer, not a flag.\n3. **The onboarding is genuinely bad.** `Scanner.swift:60-68` hardcodes seven rule paths. Two of them (`.npm/_cacache`, `.gradle/caches/build-cache-1`) are dotfolders that NSOpenPanel hides by default, and `~/Library` is hidden too — the user needs Cmd-Shift-G or Cmd-Shift-period. Most hobby cleaners install a root helper in week one and can never reach any store afterward without a rewrite. You will never have to do that rewrite. And `ROADMAP.md:28` principle 9 banning \"RAM booster / 100% safe / speeds up any Mac\" is you having independently written guideline 2.3.1(a)'s anti-pattern list before ever contemplating submission. That is not common.\n\nBut none of that helps if the app doesn't do the job. The efficacy lens measured 1.83 GB offered on this machine while the 8.2 GB DerivedData it advertises first at `README.md:32` is refused by your own `FileSafety.swift:45-54` whole-tree package/symlink policy. I want to underline the audit's sharpest point, because it's the one I'd act on first: `FileManager.trashItem` relocates by **rename** — it never dereferences symlinks inside the moved tree. The *ancestor* check in `validate` is load-bearing; the *descendant* check is guarding a threat your chosen primitive does not have, and it costs you the product. Fix: `swift build -c release --arch arm64 --arch x86_64`. Second: `Info.plist` has **zero** usage-description keys (`grep -c UsageDescription` → 0) while `MacDesktopService.swift:7-15` invites users to add project folders that can be Desktop, Documents or Downloads — all TCC-protected. ## a21b85a2c4754db96

# 1. What no lens examined

Four tracked files are never cited anywhere in the seven lens outputs or the three verdicts, and one of them hides a real store blocker.

`scripts/generate-icon.swift`, `Assets.xcassets/AppIcon.appiconset/` and `Resources/AppIcon.icns` were never read. That matters because the two build paths produce **different bundles**. `scripts/build-app.sh` builds via SwiftPM and hand-assembles the bundle at lines 7-12 — it copies only the executable, `Info.plist` and `AppIcon.icns`. I listed the shipped artifact: `build/ViggoClean.app` contains exactly four files and **no `Assets.car`**. The Xcode target's `ASSETCATALOG_COMPILER_APPICON_NAME = "AppIcon"` (project.pbxproj:64,66) applies only to the Xcode path, which is not what ships. `Info.plist` has the legacy `CFBundleIconFile` (line 9-10) and no `CFBundleIconName`. App Store Connect rejects macOS submissions whose icon is not an asset-catalog icon with `CFBundleIconName`. Two lenses discussed store readiness at length and neither found this.

`ViggoClean.xcodeproj/xcshareddata/xcschemes/ViggoClean.xcscheme` was never read. It has `LaunchAction`, `ProfileAction`, `AnalyzeAction` and `ArchiveAction` and **no `TestAction`**. ⌘U in the Xcode project runs nothing. The 32 tests exist only on the `swift test` path. That is a concrete instance of the "no CI" finding that is more actionable than the abstract version.

Bigger than any of those: **there is no `PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy` anywhere in the repo** (`find . -name "*.xcprivacy"` returns 0). No lens mentions privacy manifests at all. This app calls `attributesOfFileSystem` for free space (FileSystemServices.swift:29, disk-space category E174.1), `attributesOfItem` for modification dates (FileSafety.swift:43,57, file-timestamp category C617.1) and `UserDefaults` (PersistenceServices.swift:11-16, category CA92.1). All three are required-reason API categories. A privacy manifest is mandatory for any App Store submission. The audit answered "could it go in a store" with a long sandbox argument and missed a hard, cheap, checkable requirement.

Partially unexamined: `Tests/ViggoCleanPresentationTests/WorkspaceViewModelTests.swift` (194 lines) is referenced only as a count — no lens cites a line from it, so the claim that ViewModel coverage is good rests on the docs, not on reading. And `LocalCleaningRepository.clean` (lines 96-110) re-encodes and atomically rewrites the **entire** history array after **every single item**, with no cap on history length. Cleaning 100 items with a 1,000-record history is 100 full JSON re-encodes. Nobody flagged it.

# 2. Claims that are wrong

**The signing line number is off by one, consistently.** Every lens and every verdict cites `scripts/build-app.sh:12`. The `codesign --force --sign - --options runtime` call is on **line 13**. Line 12 is a closing `fi`.

**Scanner.swift citations drifted by 3-5 lines in two lenses.** The efficacy and skeptical-user outputs cite "Scanner.swift:43" for the dedup guard (actual **46**), ":44" for `progress(title)` (actual **47**), ":57" for the SwiftPM rule (actual **62**), ":55-63" for `developerRules` (actual **60-68**). The safety and scale lenses got the same lines right (83, 87, 157, 60-68). The finding content is correct; the citations are not, and mixed accuracy across lenses is worse than uniform error because it looks authoritative.

**"$99 enrollment" is wrong — the membership is already paid.** The distribution lens lists "a $99 enrollment" as part of the Developer ID cost. `security find-identity -v -p codesigning` shows six valid identities including `Apple Distribution: Taulant Loshi (F3X3EF7L5N)` and `Apple Distribution: Syntech Solutions AG (RSCCZ6C5P3)`. An Apple Distribution certificate cannot exist without an active paid membership. Verdict #1 caught this; the structured `confirmedMajor` and `minorFindings` did not, and they are what the author will read. The real remaining work is *creating a Developer ID Application certificate* on an account that already qualifies — closer to an hour than a weekend. This materially changes the answer to the author's second question.

**A finding is buried that should be a top-line confirmed defect.** Verdict #3 asserts that `~/Library/Caches/org.swift.swiftpm` is itself a LaunchServices package. I verified it directly: `isPackage = Optional(true)`. Because `FileSafety.snapshot` calls `walk(url)` → `record(url)` on the **root** (FileSafety.swift:82), and `record` throws at lines 51-53 when `isPackage == true`, the SwiftPM rule at Scanner.swift:62 **can never produce a candidate on any Mac with Xcode installed**. That directory is 929 MB here. Worse, `"org.swift.swiftpm"` is in the `reserved` list at Scanner.swift:79, so the generic `~/Library/Caches` child loop skips it too — 929 MB is invisible twice, by two independent mechanisms. This never appears in `confirmedMajor`, `contestedMajor` or `minorFindings`. It is the single cleanest, most universal, one-line-fixable bug in the project and the audit structurally lost it.

**Two "confirmed" findings are the same defect counted twice.** "The whole-tree package/symlink rejection..." and "On the author's own Mac the scanner refuses the 8.2 GB Xcode DerivedData tree..." are one bug with two lenses' framings. The same duplication runs through `unverifiedMajor`, where the sandbox issue appears three times, and ad-hoc signing, LICENSE and history.json each appear a second time. The defect count is inflated by roughly a third.

Things I checked and found **correct**: DerivedData is 8.2 GB with 181 symlinks and 458 `.framework`/`.bundle`/`.app` directories, so the refusal is real and universal. `ENABLE_APP_SANDBOX = "NO"` and `ENABLE_HARDENED_RUNTIME = "YES"` are at project.pbxproj:64,66 — the brief was wrong and both correcting lenses were right. `SWIFT_VERSION = "5.0"` at :63,65 is right. Git history is two commits. `codesign -dvvv` on the shipped app reports `Signature=adhoc`, `TeamIdentifier=not set`, `Internal requirements count=0`, and `spctl -a` returns `rejected` — the CRITICAL finding is fully substantiated. `FileSafety.swift:43` and `:75` really are the same `attributesOfItem` call on the same path, so the "provably redundant" round-trip is provable. `python3 scripts/generate-project.py` reproduces the committed pbxproj byte-identically, so project drift is a latent risk, not a present defect — the audit stated it correctly as risk.

# 3. Severity calibration

Directionally honest, inflated in three specific places, deflated in one.

Inflated: the O(n²) dedup at Scanner.swift:46 is marked HIGH **and** `blocksShipping: true` for a cost that requires ~5,000 candidates; the author's machine has ~120 old logs and the audit says so in the same paragraph. That is MEDIUM. The three-stats-per-entry snapshot is HIGH while the audit's own `blocksShipping` field says `false`, and ARCHITECTURE.md:68 discloses slowness on broad trees — MEDIUM. Security-scoped bookmarks are HIGH, but in a deliberately non-sandboxed app they are not a defect at all; they are a prerequisite for a channel the author has not chosen. That is informational.

The CRITICAL on ad-hoc signing is defensible *for the shipping decision* — `spctl` genuinely rejects and friends genuinely cannot open it — but ROADMAP.md:155 already scopes Developer ID signing as conditional-on-distribution work. It is an unstarted correctly-scoped task, not an undisclosed defect. HIGH-blocker reads more honestly than CRITICAL.

Deflated: the SwiftPM package bug (above) and the missing privacy manifest, neither of which is filed at all.

Against what I'd expect from a 2,523-line personal v0.2: the *engineering* findings are genuinely below the usual density for this size — no force-unwraps, no `try!`, no `@unchecked Sendable`, no shell, one destructive call. Almost every real finding is about **reach and release plumbing**, not about code quality. The audit's own scores encode that correctly (safety 8, correctness 7.5, efficacy 3). The problem is that 7 confirmed + 12 unverified + 45 minor findings *reads* like a troubled codebase, and it isn't one. The volume miscommunicates the verdict its own scores reach.

# 4. The author's two questions

**"How good is this app?"** The audit produced seven numbers between 3 and 8 and never resolved them. The honest resolution is that these measure two different things and the answer is genuinely split: the *engineering* is top-decile for a solo macOS project — Swift 6 clean, real protocol DI, one `trashItem` call, tests that assert the file still exists after a failed delete (SafetyTests.swift:74-81), no Select All anywhere, a confirmation sheet that refuses to say "freed" — while the *product* recovers under 2 GB on a 350 GB-used dev Mac. "I wrote something unusually well-built that does not yet do its job" is a coherent and useful answer; seven unreconciled scores is not.

Nobody squarely answered the implicit comparison either. Against CleanMyMac the safety posture wins and the reach loses by ~50x. Against the author's own shell aliases the reach loses by ~300x. That is the sentence the author actually needs.

**"Could it go in a store?"** The audit covers Mac App Store (no, sandbox) and Developer ID (yes). It misses three things. First, **Setapp** is never mentioned — it is the realistic "store" for a non-sandboxed Mac utility, requires Developer ID + notarization rather than the sandbox, has a well-populated cleaner category, and would sidestep the entire sandbox argument. Second, the privacy manifest and `CFBundleIconName`/`Assets.car` requirements above are hard submission blockers nobody found. Third, the membership is already paid and there is an Organization team (`Syntech Solutions AG`) available, which changes both the cost and the possible publisher identity.

# 5. The single most important next thing

Every verdict leads with "fix the package/symlink policy at FileSafety.swift:45-54." That is the right target and the wrong framing, and the framing gap is dangerous.

`FileSafety.snapshot` conflates two jobs: **measuring** a tree and **authorizing** it. Every top finding falls out of that one conflation — the DerivedData refusal, the double snapshot (Scanner.swift:49 then FileSafety.swift:103), the 3-stats-per-entry cost, the memory growth, the dead `visited` set at line 39. Loosening `record` from `throw` to `return` is not a pure relaxation: once the snapshot skips packages and symlinks, `TreeStamp` no longer describes what `trashItem` will actually move at FileSafety.swift:110. The estimate under-reports, and a `.framework` inside DerivedData gets trashed even though it was deliberately excluded from validation. Ship that as a quick patch and you have quietly broken the one property this project is built on.

So the actual next move, in order: (a) write down that the *moved* unit is the whole directory and the *measured* unit is a subset, and say so in ARCHITECTURE.md's "Filesystem policies" section which currently promises the opposite at line 57; (b) write **one** failing test with a fixture containing a `.framework` directory and one interior symlink — this is the fixture that would have caught the bug and it does not exist; (c) then change `record` to skip rather than abort, noting that `walk` needs an explicit "do not descend" signal for packages since a package is still `typeDirectory`, while symlinks self-solve because `attributesOfItem` is lstat-based and will never report `typeDirectory`; (d) fix the SwiftPM root-package case separately, because it needs a root-level exemption, not a policy change, and it is 929 MB for one line.

The audit lists the missing realistic fixture as `unverifiedMajor` and buries the design consequence entirely. It is not a coverage gap — it is the precondition for every other fix being verifiable rather than hopeful.


---

## ab23be1af55f66593

# ViggoClean 0.2.0 — Final Assessment

## Verdict

As engineering, this is roughly **8/10 against solo-developer macOS utilities** — Swift 6 clean with zero unsafe escape hatches, one destructive call in the whole codebase (`FileSafety.swift:94`, `trashItem`), and a safety test suite that asserts files *survive* failure rather than that an error was returned (`SafetyTests.swift:74-81`). As a product it is about **3/10**: on your own 512 GB Mac it offers ~1.83 GB while the 8.2 GB DerivedData it advertises first (`README.md:32`) is refused by your own safety policy, and eight shell commands you already know recover 80-120 GB. **Overall: 5/10** — an unusually well-built engine that is not yet connected to the wheels. The good news is that you did the expensive, hard-to-retrofit half first.

Can it go in a store? Not the Mac App Store as designed, and not anywhere at all today — `spctl -a -vvv -t exec build/ViggoClean.app` returns **rejected**, so even your friends cannot open it. Developer ID + notarized DMG is the realistic channel, and you are closer to it than the audit's own estimate: you already hold an active paid Apple membership.

## What this project does unusually well

**Permanent deletion is structurally impossible, not merely avoided.** There is exactly one destructive filesystem call — `FileManager.default.trashItem` at `FileSafety.swift:94`. No `removeItem`, no shell, no root helper. `SafetyTests.swift:74-81` proves the no-fallback property: when the trash operation throws, the test asserts `FileManager.default.fileExists(atPath: url.path)`. Most shipping Mac cleaners call `rm -rf` on cache paths directly.

**The containment logic is correct in the ways that actually matter.** `isInside` (`FileSafety.swift:6-9`) is component-wise on `standardized`, not a string prefix, and `../secret` is tested (`SafetyTests.swift:35`). `validate` (`:14-28`) walks every ancestor with `attributesOfItem`, which is lstat-based, so a symlinked ancestor causes refusal instead of authorising its target — with a comment at `:12-13` explaining exactly why. The scan-to-cleanup race is closed by re-snapshot and digest compare at `FileSafety.swift:103-106`.

**The tests attack the code.** `SafetyTests.swift:64-73` replaces the candidate directory with a symlink between scan and cleanup and asserts the trash closure was never called. That is a race most commercial cleaners would fail, and testing the *absence* of destruction is the only assertion shape that protects anyone.

**There is no Select All anywhere.** `ViewState.swift:23-25` and `ReviewView.swift` have no bulk-selection verb. Pre-checked Select All plus a big green button is where real-world cleaner accidents come from. The friction is the safety feature.

**Swift 6 clean with no escape hatches.** The package compiles under `swiftLanguageMode(.v6)` and `-strict-concurrency=complete` with zero warnings. Across 2,419 lines there is not one `try!`, `as!`, force unwrap, `fatalError`, `@unchecked Sendable`, `nonisolated(unsafe)`, `DispatchQueue` or semaphore. Cancellation genuinely propagates into the detached scanner worker (`FileSystemServices.swift:8-15`) at ~1 ms latency.

**It refuses to lie about its own numbers.** `ReviewView.swift:156`: "Moving to Trash does not immediately free disk space. No permanent deletion is performed." Four separate places say this. Every mainstream competitor headlines an inflated "GB freed" the instant files move. The confirmation sheet (`ReviewView.swift:129-165`) lists every full monospaced path, requires an acknowledgement toggle (`:153-155`), and deliberately gives the destructive button no `.defaultAction` keyboard shortcut while Cancel gets `.cancelAction` (`:159-161`).

**`docs/VALIDATION.md:37-45` documents per version what was *not* tested.** I have not seen a commercial product do that.

## What is actually wrong

Ordered by how much it matters. A note on calibration first: the raw audit produced 7 "confirmed" + 12 "unverified" + 45 minor findings, but several are the same defect under different lenses (the DerivedData refusal appears twice; sandbox three times; LICENSE, signing and history.json twice each). The real defect count is roughly a third lower than it reads, and almost every finding is about **reach and release plumbing**, not code quality.

**1. Ad-hoc signing — the only thing that is broken for you right now.** `scripts/build-app.sh:13` runs `codesign --force --sign - --options runtime`. Verified: `flags=0x10002(adhoc,runtime)`, `TeamIdentifier=not set`, `Internal requirements count=0`, and `spctl -a` returns **rejected**. Two consequences. A friend who receives the app with a quarantine xattr gets "cannot be opened"; on macOS 15+ the right-click-Open bypass is gone. And because the designated requirement falls back to a bare cdhash, every rebuild is a new app identity, so any Full Disk Access grant silently dies and the next scan quietly finds less with no error. These are one fix, not two. *Partially disclosed* — `ROADMAP.md:155` scopes Developer ID signing as conditional future work, but nothing records that the current state is Gatekeeper-blocked. Effort: an afternoon.

**2. The whole-tree package/symlink rejection kills the flagship feature.** `FileSafety.swift:45-47` (link/special), `:51-53` (package) and `:58-60` (hard link) abort the *entire* candidate on the first offending entry. On your machine that refuses DerivedData (8.2 GB, 181 symlinks, 458 `.framework`/`.bundle`/`.app` dirs), `~/Library/Caches/Google` (6.3 GB), JetBrains (1.5 GB), Spotify (1.3 GB) and SwiftPM (929 MB). 91% of the bytes your own rules nominated are discarded by your own policy, and it shows up in the UI as a "skipped location". Note the asymmetry: the *ancestor* check in `validate` is load-bearing; the *descendant* symlink check guards a threat `trashItem` does not have, since a rename-based move never dereferences links inside the moved tree — your own `SafetyTests.swift:42` asserts `secret.txt` survives, which it would have anyway. *Disclosed as a policy* in `ARCHITECTURE.md`; **not disclosed** is that it means DerivedData never works. Effort: see the caveat under "What I would do next" — not the "hours" the audit claims.

**3. The SwiftPM rule can never fire on any Mac, and this was nearly lost.** `~/Library/Caches/org.swift.swiftpm` has `pathExtension == "swiftpm"`, a LaunchServices-registered package type, so `isPackage == true` for the candidate **root itself**. `snapshot` calls `walk(url)` → `record(url)` (`FileSafety.swift:82, 74`), which throws at `:51-53` before touching a single child. The rule at `Scanner.swift:62` is dead by construction. Worse, `"org.swift.swiftpm"` is also in the `reserved` list at `Scanner.swift:79`, so the generic Library/Caches loop skips it too — 929 MB invisible twice by two independent mechanisms. **Undisclosed.** This is the cleanest one-line-fixable bug in the repo, and it needs a root-level exemption, not a policy change. Effort: minutes.

**4. No diagnosability at all.** Zero logging statements in the entire source tree (verified by grep: 0 hits for `os.Logger`, `OSLog`, `NSLog`, `print(`). Every error is flattened to `localizedDescription` (`FileSafety.swift:115`, plus ~15 sites across Scanner, repository and ViewModel), so the app structurally cannot distinguish "macOS denied permission" from "the file changed" — the single most useful thing a disk cleaner can say. No support contact (`WorkspaceScreens.swift:121`), no diagnostics export, no update mechanism. Your no-telemetry stance is right; it has quietly become no-diagnosability. **Undisclosed.** Effort: days.

**5. `history.json` has no schema version and fails closed permanently.** `LocalCleaningRepository.swift:30-31` sets `historyAvailable = false` on any decode error, and `:74-75` then refuses all cleanup. `CleanupRecord` (`Models.swift:85-96`) has no version field, and there is no in-app recovery — the user must hand-delete `~/Library/Application Support/ViggoClean/history.json`. Shipping 0.3 with any model change bricks the Clean button for every 0.2 user. The fail-closed choice is *disclosed and correct* (`ARCHITECTURE.md:31`); the absence of a recovery path is **undisclosed**. Effort: hours. Related and unflagged: `:104` re-encodes and atomically rewrites the entire history array after **every single item**, with no cap on history length.

**6. No LICENSE, no warranty disclaimer anywhere.** `ls LICENSE*` returns nothing; grep for `warranty|disclaim|liab` across the repo returns nothing. For a tool that moves other people's files to Trash, this is the cheapest serious gap you have. **Undisclosed.** Effort: ten minutes.

**7. Missing store-submission artifacts nobody in the audit caught.** There is no `PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy` anywhere (`find` returns 0), yet you call required-reason APIs: `attributesOfFileSystem` (`FileSystemServices.swift:29`), `attributesOfItem` (`FileSafety.swift:43`) and `UserDefaults` (`PersistenceServices.swift:11-16`). A privacy manifest is mandatory for any App Store submission. Separately, `build/ViggoClean.app` contains exactly four files and **no `Assets.car`** — the SwiftPM build path (`build-app.sh:7-11`) copies only the executable, Info.plist and `.icns`, and `Info.plist:9-10` uses legacy `CFBundleIconFile` with no `CFBundleIconName`. App Store Connect rejects that. Also missing: `LSApplicationCategoryType`, and any TCC usage-description keys even though `MacDesktopService` invites users to add Desktop/Documents/Downloads folders.

**8. Arm64-only binary.** `build-app.sh:4` runs `swift build -c release` with no `--arch` flags; `lipo -info` confirms `Non-fat file: architecture: arm64`. `Info.plist:21` promises macOS 14.0+, which reads as "any Sonoma Mac". A friend on an Intel MacBook gets a bundle that cannot launch. **Undisclosed.** Effort: one flag.

**9. `SWIFT_VERSION = "5.0"` in both Xcode configurations** (`project.pbxproj:63, 65`), the shared scheme has **no `TestAction`** (⌘U runs nothing), and there is no CI. Nothing preserves the Swift 6 cleanliness the code currently has.

### Contested and unverified

**The memory finding is half right.** `FileSafety.swift:73-81` has no `autoreleasepool` and peak growth is real and reproducible (~12.5 KB/entry on a 50k tree; 2,555 MB on 200k; a one-line per-child `autoreleasepool` drops that to 25.8 MB and is *faster*). But two claimed impacts were **refuted by measurement**: memory *is* released at the detached-task boundary (`FileSystemServices.swift:8-10`), and the headline "500k-entry DerivedData → 9 GB" scenario cannot occur, because `record` refuses DerivedData in the first few milliseconds — the real snapshot peaked at 21 MB RSS. Fix it (one line, strictly faster), but it is not the crisis it was filed as.

**The O(n²) dedup at `Scanner.swift:46` was filed as a shipping blocker; I do not agree.** 87 s at 5,000 candidates is real, but your machine has ~120 old logs. MEDIUM, not blocking.

**Never verified by anyone:** the VoiceOver findings (the Review list is ~1,100 ungrouped stops, no announcements after a cleanup), the accessibility/Dynamic Type findings, and the claim that the ViewModel test coverage is good — it was counted, never read.

## The efficacy problem

Direct answer: **no, it does not currently solve your problem.**

Measured on this machine (460 GiB volume, ~350 GiB used, 81-84% full), the real scanner offers **1.83-1.89 GB** — about 0.5% of used disk. Of your seven developer rules (`Scanner.swift:60-68`), four refuse and three point at paths that do not exist here, delivering ~563 MB combined.

What is actually sitting on the disk, measured with `du`: 39 GB `/Library/Developer/CoreSimulator` runtimes, 34 GB `~/.android`, 26 GB Docker VM, 26 GB `/opt/homebrew`, 22 GB `~/Library/Android`, 12.1 GB node_modules, 9.9 GB `~/.gradle` (your rule reaches 494 MB of it), 8.2 GB DerivedData, 6.4 GB CoreSimulator devices, 5.6 GB iOS DeviceSupport. Roughly 200 GB addressable.

Three structural reasons the gap is this wide, beyond the package/symlink policy:
- Scanning is confined to `$HOME` (`Scanner.swift:7, 20`), so the two largest items are invisible by construction.
- `node_modules` is deliberately pruned from project traversal (`Scanner.swift:157`).
- The rule catalogue is seven hardcoded `(String, String, String?)` tuples inlined in a 145-line function. Free competitors ship 30-39 scanners.

Fixing the package/symlink policy unlocks roughly 17 GB immediately. That is a 10x improvement and still an order of magnitude short of what a shell session gets you. The rule-coverage work is months, not hours.

## App Store reality

**Mac App Store: closed, for reasons that are product-shaped, not budget-shaped.** `ENABLE_APP_SANDBOX = "NO"` at `project.pbxproj:64` and `:66` is a deliberate, correct choice, not an omission. The concrete failure mode, measured with signed probe apps: under the sandbox, `FileManager.default.homeDirectoryForCurrentUser` returns `~/Library/Containers/<bundleid>/Data`, so `Scanner.swift:7` and `AppContainer.swift:12` would scan an empty folder the app created itself and report **zero candidates and zero errors** — a confident, silent "your Mac is clean". That is the worst failure mode a cleaner can have.

One audit claim needs correcting: "a sandboxed app cannot read other apps' `~/Library/Caches` at all" is too strong. A probe signed with `temporary-exception.files.home-relative-path.read-write` read the real paths fine. The sandbox forbids reading them *without an explicit grant of that subtree*; security-scoped bookmarks grant the same thing legitimately. `NSWorkspace.runningApplications` and real volume capacity also survive sandboxing. So MAS is technically reachable — it is just the wrong trade: the temporary-exception route is exactly what App Review has refused for a decade, and the bookmark route means rewriting persistence (`PersistenceServices.swift:11-16` stores bare path strings, which are inert on relaunch under sandbox) plus a grant-onboarding flow where two of your seven rules point at dotfolders NSOpenPanel hides by default.

Precedent cuts both ways. DevCleaner for Xcode is free on the MAS, sandboxed, minOS 14.0, and its store copy pitches "especially important if you have relatively small SSD drive" — your sentence, at $0, since 2018. But it survives because it is framed as an *Xcode developer tool*, not a Mac cleaner, and it had to **remove** its UI Previews feature because sandbox permissions could not reach those files. DaisyDisk's store build ships without scan-as-administrator for the same reason. (One judge flagged that the DevCleaner listing could not be re-verified this session — treat it as recalled precedent.)

**Recommended channel: Developer ID + notarized, stapled DMG.** Not MAS, not now. Setapp Desktop is the one "store" that fits a non-sandboxed utility and takes 10-15%, but it wants a mature differentiated app; it is not where this goes next.

**Where the three commercial judges disagreed.** All three converged on the channel; they split on money and on cost. The release engineer verified `security find-identity -v` and found a valid **Apple Distribution: Taulant Loshi (F3X3EF7L5N)** certificate — you already hold an active paid membership, so the "$99 enrollment" in the audit and in the monetization judge's verdict is **wrong**; what is missing is a free Developer ID Application certificate, five minutes from an account that already qualifies. The monetization judge argued for permanently free and open-source (differentiator is auditability, which only works in public; free competitors already claim your exact safety language in their marketing copy). The skeptical user agreed on free but framed it as "not yet good enough to charge", leaving the door open. They also split on effort for the `FileSafety` fix: the audit said hours, the skeptical user said a couple of weekends and warned that rushing it is how you destroy the one thing this project has. I side with the latter.

## If you want to ship this

### (A) Minimum to give it to friends safely

Ordered by dependency.

1. **LICENSE with a warranty disclaimer** at the repo root — 10 minutes. Do this before the next `.dmg` leaves your machine.
2. **SwiftPM root-package exemption** (`Scanner.swift:62` + remove `"org.swift.swiftpm"` from `reserved` at `:79`) — 30 minutes, 929 MB.
3. **A realistic cache fixture**: one tree with a `.framework` directory, one interior symlink, one hard link, a nested excluded path — half a day. This is the precondition for item 4 being verifiable rather than hopeful.
4. **Per-entry package/symlink handling in `FileSafety.record`** (`:45-53`), dropping the descendant-symlink rule and keeping the ancestor check in `validate` — 1-2 weekends including fixtures. See the caveat in the next section.
5. **Developer ID Application certificate, then `build-app.sh:13` → `--sign "Developer ID Application: Taulant Loshi (F3X3EF7L5N)" --timestamp`, plus `--arch arm64 --arch x86_64` on line 4, then `notarytool submit --wait` and `stapler staple`** — one afternoon. Notarize the DMG and staple the DMG; verify with `spctl -a -vvv -t install`. Your bundle is 4 files and 2.5 MB with zero third-party frameworks; it will pass first try.
6. **Schema version on `CleanupRecord`** (`Models.swift:85-96`) + a "Reset activity history" button in Settings — half a day. Must land before any 0.3.
7. **`os.Logger` at `FileSafety.swift:113-116` and `LocalCleaningRepository.swift:96-110`, plus typed errors instead of `localizedDescription`** — 1-2 days.
8. **Three TCC usage-description keys in Info.plist** and a Full Disk Access explainer with an `x-apple.systempreferences:` deep link — half a day.
9. **One-line `autoreleasepool` in `walk`** (`FileSafety.swift:79`) — 15 minutes, and it makes scans faster.

Realistic total: 4-6 weeks at 8-10 hours a week.

### (B) Additional work to sell it to strangers

1. **Extract the rule catalogue** out of the 145-line `scan` function into a versioned, per-rule structure with its own reason and consequence strings — 3-5 days. `ROADMAP.md` Phase 2 cannot land on the current tuple array.
2. **Rules that reach the real bytes**: CoreSimulator runtimes and devices, iOS DeviceSupport, Xcode Archives, subdirectory-aware `~/.gradle`, Android SDK/AVD, a Docker adapter that talks to the daemon rather than deleting a 26 GB VM file, Homebrew via `brew --cache` — 6-10 weeks with fixtures per rule as `AGENTS.md:11` requires.
3. **Decide about scanning outside `$HOME`** and build the Full Disk Access onboarding that implies — 1-2 weeks.
4. **Replace the card list with a real `Table`** (`ReviewView.swift:51-56`): sortable columns, native multi-select and shift-click range, while keeping your deliberate no-Select-All stance — 1 week. Highest-leverage single change for making it read as a Mac app.
5. **Accessibility pass**: VoiceOver announcements for scan/cleanup outcomes, grouped rows, Dynamic Type, focus indication — 1-2 weeks. Currently a VoiceOver user gets silence after confirming a 30 GB delete.
6. **Update channel** (Sparkle or a dumb hosted JSON version check) — 3-5 days. Without it you cannot push a safety fix to anyone who already installed.
7. **CI + a `TestAction` in the shared scheme + `SWIFT_VERSION = 6.0`** — 1 day.
8. **Distinguish failure from success in the UI** (`ContentView.swift:23-32` renders "Scan failed" in the same mint info banner as "12 items moved to Trash") — half a day.
9. Only if pursuing MAS: security-scoped bookmark persistence, entitlements file, `PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy`, asset-catalog icon with `CFBundleIconName`, `LSApplicationCategoryType`, review notes declaring the `--preview-data` flag — 2-3 months, for a deliberately worse product.

## What I would do next

**1. Reframe `FileSafety.snapshot` before you touch it.** Every top finding falls out of one conflation: the function both *measures* a tree and *authorizes* it. Loosening `record` from `throw` to `return` is not a pure relaxation — once the snapshot skips packages and symlinks, `TreeStamp` no longer describes what `trashItem` actually moves at `FileSafety.swift:110`. The estimate under-reports, and a `.framework` inside DerivedData gets trashed even though it was deliberately excluded from validation. Write down that the *moved* unit is the whole directory and the *measured* unit is a subset, correct `ARCHITECTURE.md` where it currently promises the opposite, then build the realistic fixture, *then* change the code. Note that `walk` will need an explicit "do not descend" signal for packages (a package is still `typeDirectory`), while symlinks self-solve because `attributesOfItem` is lstat-based. Do the SwiftPM root exemption separately in the same session — it is one line for 929 MB.

**2. Sign it properly and give it a licence.** One afternoon, and it fixes Gatekeeper and the TCC-reset problem simultaneously. The membership is already paid; you need a free Developer ID Application certificate, `--timestamp`, `--arch arm64 --arch x86_64`, notarytool, stapler, and a LICENSE file. Right now your friends cannot open the app at all, and on an Intel Mac they never will.

**3. Make failures visible, then publish the source.** `os.Logger`, typed errors instead of `localizedDescription`, a diagnostics export, a contact address, and a version check. Then put it on GitHub under MIT or Apache-2.0 — not Apache + Commons Clause, which is the licence that helped strand Pearcleaner. Your differentiator is that the safety claims are *checkable*: one `trashItem` call, no Select All, a test that proves the file survives a failed delete. That is a real position in the most trust-hostile category on the platform, and it is worth exactly nothing from an unknown developer shipping a closed binary. Publishing the repo is the marketing.

Do not price it yet. Charging $29 for 1.83 GB would burn the only asset this project actually has.
